      ABOUT THE COMPANIES
     </p>
     <h1>
      Inspection support from India and the UAE.
     </h1>
     <p class="lead">
      IAIS India and IAIS UAE support industrial inspection requirements through teams in Chennai and Abu Dhabi. This website presents their focused TOFD, PAUT, TFM and FMC inspection offering for clients with clearly defined technical objectives.
     </p>

     </h2>
    </div>

     <p>
      <strong>
       Integrity &amp; Advanced Inspection Solutions India Pvt. Ltd.
      </strong>
      is based in the Chennai region.

       Integrity &amp; Advanced Inspection Solutions UAE LLC
      </strong>
      is based in Abu Dhabi. The companies' wider inspection services are described on their respective corporate websites.
     </p>
     <p>
      For this specialist website, enquiries begin with the component, the inspection objective and the applicable requirements. These details help determine whether an advanced ultrasonic method is suitable and what should be included in the proposed scope.
     </p>
    </div>

       IAIS TODAY
      </p>
      <h2>
       Industrial inspection with an advanced ultrasonic focus.
      </h2>
      <p>
       IAIS India&rsquo;s wider published portfolio includes conventional and advanced nondestructive testing, in-service inspection, marine and offshore inspection, and related industrial technical services. IAIS UAE provides a regional point of contact for inspection and asset-integrity needs in Abu Dhabi and the wider UAE.
      </p>
      <p>
       This site gives TOFD, PAUT, TFM and FMC their own clear technical context. The methods can be discussed individually or as part of a project-specific inspection plan, depending on geometry, material, access and the governing requirements.
      </p>

     </h2>
    </div>
    <div>
     <h3>
      Advanced ultrasonic services
     </h3>
     <p>
      TOFD and PAUT examinations, with FMC acquisition and TFM imaging considered where they suit the inspection objective and approved procedure.
     </p>
     <h3>
      Wider IAIS services
     </h3>
     <p>
      The companies&rsquo; corporate sites describe additional inspection, testing and asset-integrity services. This specialist site remains focused on advanced ultrasonic inspection and links to those sites for the full portfolio.
     </p>
     <h3>
      Project delivery
     </h3>
     <p>
      Each enquiry is reviewed for component details, required coverage, applicable standard, site conditions, personnel and equipment needs. The final scope and schedule are agreed for the assignment.
     </p>
    </div>
   </section>
   <!-- ===== CONTENT SECTION 5: about-band ===== -->
   <section class="about-band">
    <div class="wrap">
     <p class="section-kicker">
      PROJECT DISCUSSION
     </p>
     <h2>
      What helps us understand your requirement?
     </h2>
     <div class="about-items">
      <article>
       <span>
        01
       </span>
       <h3>
        Asset and geometry
       </h3>
       <p>
        Component type, weld configuration, material, thickness and access conditions.
       </p>
      </article>
      <article>
       <span>
        02
       </span>
       <h3>
        Inspection objective
       </h3>
       <p>
        Fabrication examination, in-service assessment, indication characterization or another defined purpose.
       </p>
      </article>
      <article>
       <span>
        03
       </span>
       <h3>
        Acceptance and delivery
       </h3>
       <p>
        Applicable code or specification, reporting expectations, site location and target schedule.
       </p>
      </article>
     </div>
     <p class="about-footnote">
      The above is an enquiry checklist, not a statement that every technique is appropriate for every component.
     </p>
    </div>

     <p class="eyebrow">
      APPLICATIONS &amp; INSPECTION PLANNING
     </p>
     <h1>
      Start with the asset. Build the inspection around the decision.
     </h1>
     <p class="lead">
      A weld, pipe or pressure component does not need a technique by name; it needs a defined examination volume and a result that supports a decision. Use this guide to identify the likely method options and the information IAIS needs to assess your application.
     </p>
     <div class="actions">
      <a class="button primary" href="#application-guide">
       Explore applications
       <span aria-hidden="true">
        &darr;
       </span>
      </a>
      <a class="button secondary" href="#enquiry">
       Discuss your component
       <span aria-hidden="true">
        &nearrow;
       </span>
      </a>
     </div>
    </div>
   </section>
   <!-- ===== CONTENT SECTION 2: application-intro wrap ===== -->
   <section class="application-intro wrap">
    <div>
     <p class="section-kicker">
      HOW TO USE THIS GUIDE
     </p>
     <h2>
      Match the inspection question to the component and conditions.
     </h2>
    </div>
    <div>
     <p>
      The same asset can require different ultrasonic strategies. A fabrication weld may need volumetric examination against a construction code. An operating line may need a targeted assessment of an indication or a map of remaining wall thickness. A complex material may need a specially demonstrated procedure before the method can be applied with confidence.
     </p>
     <p>
      The examples below are
      <strong>
       starting points for a technical discussion
      </strong>
      . The final technique, probe arrangement, calibration, coverage, personnel and acceptance basis must follow the governing specification and project procedure.
     </p>
    </div>
   </section>

     HTHA assessment
    </a>

     More applications
    </a>
   </nav>
   <!-- ===== CONTENT SECTION 3: application-guide ===== -->
   <section class="application-guide" id="application-guide">
    <div class="wrap">
     <p class="section-kicker">
      APPLICATION GUIDE
     </p>
     <h2>
      Common inspection questions and possible approaches
     </h2>
     <article class="application-case" id="fabrication-welds">
      <div class="case-label">
       <span>
        01 / FABRICATION
       </span>
       <h3>
        New welds and construction joints
       </h3>
       <p>
        Pipe girth welds, vessel seams, structural welds and similar fabricated joints.
       </p>
      </div>
      <div class="case-body">
       <h4>
        What the client needs to know
       </h4>
       <p>
        Has the required weld volume been examined, where are any reportable indications, and how are they evaluated against the specified acceptance criteria? The answer depends on joint configuration, weld preparation, thickness, material and the applicable construction specification.
       </p>
       <h4>
        Methods to discuss
       </h4>
       <p>
        <a href="paut.html">
         PAUT
        </a>
        can provide planned angle-beam coverage and encoded views.
        <a href="tofd.html">
         TOFD
        </a>
        can add diffraction-based information and through-wall sizing in suitable welds. A combined examination may help address different response mechanisms, particularly around areas where one method has a coverage limitation.
       </p>
       <h4>
        What to send IAIS
       </h4>
       <p>
        Weld map, joint details, nominal thickness, material grade, weld cap condition, examination stage, applicable code and access on each side of the weld.
       </p>
       <p class="case-caution">
        <strong>
         Check before selecting:
        </strong>
        root and cap coverage, probe travel, surface preparation, calibration reflectors and any restrictions from the governing procedure.
       </p>
      </div>
     </article>
     <article class="application-case" id="in-service-piping">
      <div class="case-label">
       <span>
        02 / OPERATIONS
       </span>
       <h3>
        In-service piping and weld assessment
       </h3>
       <p>
        Accessible process lines, circumferential welds, elbows and connections during service or shutdown.
       </p>
      </div>
      <div class="case-body">
       <h4>
        What the client needs to know
       </h4>
       <p>
        Is a known or suspected indication present, how does it relate to the weld or adjacent base material, and what information can support engineering review? Operating history, likely damage mechanism and prior inspection findings are often as important as the nominal pipe size.
       </p>
       <h4>
        Methods to discuss
       </h4>
       <p>
        <a href="paut.html">
         PAUT
        </a>
        may be used for targeted weld examination with probes and scanners chosen for diameter and access.
        <a href="tofd.html">
         TOFD
        </a>
        may be considered where probe spacing and surface geometry permit a suitable scan.
        <a href="tfm.html">
         TFM
        </a>
        can be assessed for a specific region of interest when an imaging procedure is validated.
       </p>
       <h4>
        What to send IAIS
       </h4>
       <p>
        Pipe OD and wall thickness, material, weld profile, insulation or coating status, temperature, access restrictions, previous reports and the question the inspection must answer.
       </p>
       <p class="case-caution">
        <strong>
         Check before selecting:
        </strong>
        small diameters, elbows, nozzles, adjacent fittings and limited scan clearance can change the usable probe setup and coverage.
       </p>
      </div>
     </article>
     <article class="application-case" id="pressure-equipment">
      <div class="case-label">
       <span>
        03 / EQUIPMENT
       </span>
       <h3>
        Pressure vessels, reactors and tanks
       </h3>
       <p>
        Shell welds, longitudinal and circumferential seams, selected nozzles and accessible pressure boundaries.
       </p>
      </div>
      <div class="case-body">
       <h4>
        What the client needs to know
       </h4>
       <p>
        Does the examination concern weld quality, an in-service indication, remaining wall thickness or a specific degradation mechanism? These are different scopes. The inspection zone, material and physical access determine whether one ultrasonic method or several complementary examinations are needed.
       </p>
       <h4>
        Methods to discuss
       </h4>
       <p>
        <a href="paut.html">
         PAUT
        </a>
        and
        <a href="tofd.html">
         TOFD
        </a>
        can be considered for suitable weld seams. For a localized indication that needs further imaging,
        <a href="tfm.html">
         TFM
        </a>
        with
        <a href="fmc.html">
         FMC
        </a>
        data may be assessed. Broad-area wall-loss mapping calls for a dedicated thickness-mapping setup rather than assuming a weld scan will provide it.
       </p>
       <h4>
        What to send IAIS
       </h4>
       <p>
        Equipment drawings, weld identification, material and cladding details, nominal thickness, inspection history, operating conditions, access plan and the governing inspection requirement.
       </p>
       <p class="case-caution">
        <strong>
         Check before selecting:
        </strong>
        curvature, cladding, coarse-grained weld metal, internals and site conditions can affect sound propagation and scan coverage.
       </p>
      </div>
     </article>
     <article class="application-case" id="corrosion-mapping">
      <div class="case-label">
       <span>
        04 / WALL LOSS
       </span>
       <h3>
        Corrosion and thickness mapping
       </h3>
       <p>
        Defined areas of pipe, vessel or tank wall where remaining thickness or localized metal loss is the question.
       </p>
      </div>
      <div class="case-body">
       <h4>
        What the client needs to know
       </h4>
       <p>
        Where has material been lost, how extensive is it, and what remaining-wall data are needed for an engineering assessment? A useful mapping scope defines the area, grid or encoded coverage, minimum reportable thickness and how data will be tied to the asset location.
       </p>
       <h4>
        Methods to discuss
       </h4>
       <p>
        A suitably configured
        <a href="paut.html">
         phased-array thickness-mapping
        </a>
        approach may produce a mapped record of the examined area. Localized conventional ultrasonic thickness measurements or another technique may also be required under the project plan.
        <a href="tofd.html">
         TOFD
        </a>
        is primarily discussed here for specific weld-root questions, not as a general substitute for broad-area corrosion mapping.
       </p>
       <h4>
        What to send IAIS
       </h4>
       <p>
        Asset drawing and location map, expected corrosion area, nominal thickness, material, coating or insulation condition, surface temperature and required grid or resolution.
       </p>
       <p class="case-caution">
        <strong>
         Check before selecting:
        </strong>
        coating, surface profile, pitting geometry, inaccessible areas and the coupling arrangement can affect the reliability of thickness data.
       </p>
      </div>
     </article>
     <article class="application-case" id="complex-indications">
      <div class="case-label">
       <span>
        05 / INVESTIGATION
       </span>
       <h3>
        Complex indications and difficult geometry
       </h3>
       <p>
        Questions involving indication orientation, location, sizing or material response that need more than a routine scan.
       </p>
      </div>
      <div class="case-body">
       <h4>
        What the client needs to know
       </h4>
       <p>
        What uncertainty remains after the initial inspection? The aim may be to locate an indication within the weld volume, estimate its extent or test a suspected damage mechanism. The technique should be chosen to answer that exact question, with reference reflectors and a validation plan where needed.
       </p>
       <h4>
        Methods to discuss
       </h4>
       <p>
        <a href="paut.html">
         PAUT
        </a>
        can provide complementary beam angles and scan views.
        <a href="tofd.html">
         TOFD
        </a>
        can contribute diffraction information.
        <a href="fmc.html">
         FMC
        </a>
        acquisition and
        <a href="tfm.html">
         TFM
        </a>
        reconstruction may provide a more focused image for a defined region of interest. More image detail does not automatically improve detection for every geometry.
       </p>
       <h4>
        What to send IAIS
       </h4>
       <p>
        Raw or reported prior inspection findings where available, suspected indication position, material and weld details, access constraints and the decision the new examination must support.
       </p>
       <p class="case-caution">
        <strong>
         Check before selecting:
        </strong>
        material velocity, attenuation, mode selection, aperture, focal region and reference block response require technical review.
       </p>
      </div>
     </article>
     <article class="application-case" id="htha">
      <div class="case-label">
       <span>
        06 / SPECIALIST
       </span>
       <h3>
        High-temperature hydrogen attack assessment
       </h3>
       <p>
        A specialist inspection question for relevant equipment exposed to hydrogen at elevated temperature.
       </p>
      </div>
      <div class="case-body">
       <h4>
        What the client needs to know
       </h4>
       <p>
        Is the asset susceptible under its material and operating history, and what locations require examination? HTHA assessment is not resolved by a generic ultrasonic scan. Inspection planning should account for temperature and pressure history, material, welds and heat-affected zones, the expected damage stage and the engineering decision.
       </p>
       <h4>
        Methods to discuss
       </h4>
       <p>
        Focused
        <a href="paut.html">
         PAUT
        </a>
        ,
        <a href="tofd.html">
         TOFD
        </a>
        and
        <a href="tfm.html">
         TFM
        </a>
        may be considered as complementary ultrasonic approaches under a validated HTHA-specific procedure.
        <a href="fmc.html">
         FMC
        </a>
        may support the TFM acquisition. Technique performance needs demonstration on relevant reference or validation samples for the proposed scope.
       </p>
       <h4>
        What to send IAIS
       </h4>
       <p>
        Material and fabrication records, service conditions, operating history, previous inspection data, component dimensions, weld locations and the applicable integrity assessment requirements.
       </p>
       <p class="case-caution">
        <strong>
         Check before selecting:
        </strong>
        early damage can be difficult to detect. A negative ultrasonic result should not be presented as proof that HTHA is absent; engineering assessment and the approved inspection program remain essential.
       </p>
      </div>
     </article>
    </div>
   </section>
   <!-- ===== CONTENT SECTION 4: more-applications ===== -->
   <section class="expanded-applications" id="more-applications">
    <div class="wrap">
     <p class="section-kicker">
      EXTENDED APPLICATION LIBRARY
     </p>
     <h2>
      More components and inspection questions
     </h2>
     <p class="expanded-intro">
      Ultrasonic techniques can be adapted to many components, but their use is never universal. The examples below explain what a client may ask for, which methods are worth evaluating and what would be needed to develop a defensible scope.
     </p>
     <nav aria-label="More application categories" class="expanded-index">
      <a href="#weld-configurations">
       Weld configurations
      </a>
      <a href="#operating-assets">
       Operating assets
      </a>
      <a href="#specialist-components">
       Specialist components
      </a>
     </nav>
    </div>
    <div class="expanded-group wrap" id="weld-configurations">
     <div class="expanded-group-head">
      <h3>
       Weld and fabrication configurations
      </h3>
      <p>
       The weld configuration determines probe placement, sound path and scan coverage. These examples describe technology possibilities; the governing code and a demonstrated procedure determine the actual examination.
      </p>
     </div>
     <div class="expanded-grid">
      <article class="expanded-card">
       <h4>
        Pipe girth welds
       </h4>
       <p>
        <strong>
         Inspection question.
        </strong>
        Determine whether the full circumferential weld volume and heat-affected zones can be covered, including the root and cap. Diameter, wall thickness and nearby fittings drive scanner and wedge selection.
       </p>
       <p>
        <strong>
         Methods to assess.
        </strong>
        PAUT is often the starting point; TOFD may add diffraction-based sizing when both sides of the weld are accessible. For small diameter pipe, a dedicated scanner and coverage demonstration may be necessary.
       </p>
       <p>
        <strong>
         Scope inputs.
        </strong>
        Pipe OD, schedule or measured thickness, weld profile, access around the full circumference and required acceptance basis.
       </p>
      </article>
      <article class="expanded-card">
       <h4>
        Longitudinal pipe and vessel seams
       </h4>
       <p>
        <strong>
         Inspection question.
        </strong>
        Examine a long axial weld with a repeatable scan position and traceable location along its length. Curvature and plate thickness influence the sound path.
       </p>
       <p>
        <strong>
         Methods to assess.
        </strong>
        Encoded PAUT and, where geometry suits, TOFD may be considered. The scan plan must define both sides of the weld and any changes near seam intersections.
       </p>
       <p>
        <strong>
         Scope inputs.
        </strong>
        Seam length, shell diameter, weld drawing, thickness, seam crossings and access to the examination surface.
       </p>
      </article>
      <article class="expanded-card">
       <h4>
        Nozzle, branch and attachment welds
       </h4>
       <p>
        <strong>
         Inspection question.
        </strong>
        Check welds whose local curvature, reinforcement and intersecting geometries make standard straight-weld scans difficult.
       </p>
       <p>
        <strong>
         Methods to assess.
        </strong>
        PAUT may be adapted using multiple probe positions or specially designed wedges. TFM can be considered for a defined region after validation. TOFD requires a feasible probe-pair path and should not be assumed suitable around a nozzle.
       </p>
       <p>
        <strong>
         Scope inputs.
        </strong>
        Nozzle orientation and diameter, attachment profile, thickness transitions, access and the exact weld volume of interest.
       </p>
      </article>
      <article class="expanded-card">
       <h4>
        Thick-section and heavy fabrication welds
       </h4>
       <p>
        <strong>
         Inspection question.
        </strong>
        Plan adequate depth coverage and sensitivity through thick material, where one probe arrangement may not cover the whole volume.
       </p>
       <p>
        <strong>
         Methods to assess.
        </strong>
        Multiple PAUT scan plans and TOFD zones may be required. TFM/FMC can be evaluated for a targeted indication, with sound paths and imaging zone demonstrated on representative material.
       </p>
       <p>
        <strong>
         Scope inputs.
        </strong>
        Section thickness, material and heat treatment, weld preparation, reference block and any prior indications.
       </p>
      </article>
      <article class="expanded-card">
       <h4>
        Dissimilar metal, stainless and clad welds
       </h4>
       <p>
        <strong>
         Inspection question.
        </strong>
        Address beam distortion, attenuation and backscatter that may arise in coarse-grained or anisotropic weld metal or at an interface.
       </p>
       <p>
        <strong>
         Methods to assess.
        </strong>
        Specialized PAUT probe modes and qualified setups may be explored. TOFD and TFM are not automatic substitutes; each requires evidence of performance in the actual material and geometry.
       </p>
       <p>
        <strong>
         Scope inputs.
        </strong>
        Base and filler metals, cladding or overlay thickness, weld procedure, grain structure where known, and representative calibration material.
       </p>
      </article>
     </div>
    </div>
    <div class="expanded-group wrap" id="operating-assets">
     <div class="expanded-group-head">
      <h3>
       Operating assets and degradation
      </h3>
      <p>
       For an operating asset, the suspected damage mechanism should be stated before selecting a scan. Weld examination, metal-loss mapping and crack investigation need different setups.
      </p>
     </div>
     <div class="expanded-grid">
      <article class="expanded-card">
       <h4>
        Pipe elbows and curved surfaces
       </h4>
       <p>
        <strong>
         Inspection question.
        </strong>
        Locate and measure thinning or erosion at the intrados, extrados and downstream regions of an elbow. The curved surface changes contact and scan tracking.
       </p>
       <p>
        <strong>
         Methods to assess.
        </strong>
        A flexible-array or otherwise qualified PAUT thickness-mapping setup may be suitable. A conventional weld PAUT scan or TOFD scan does not provide the same area map.
       </p>
       <p>
        <strong>
         Scope inputs.
        </strong>
        Elbow radius, diameter, wall thickness, material, expected thinning region, insulation status and requested map resolution.
       </p>
      </article>
      <article class="expanded-card">
       <h4>
        Weld-root corrosion and erosion
       </h4>
       <p>
        <strong>
         Inspection question.
        </strong>
        Investigate localized metal loss near a weld root or adjacent heat-affected zone, where geometry can complicate a simple thickness reading.
       </p>
       <p>
        <strong>
         Methods to assess.
        </strong>
        Targeted TOFD or PAUT may be assessed against a specific inspection objective and suitable reference sample. Complementary wall-thickness measurements may be needed to quantify remaining material.
       </p>
       <p>
        <strong>
         Scope inputs.
        </strong>
        Weld profile, access, nominal and prior thickness, process direction, prior inspection data and suspected affected length.
       </p>
      </article>
      <article class="expanded-card">
       <h4>
        Tank and vessel shell wall loss
       </h4>
       <p>
        <strong>
         Inspection question.
        </strong>
        Produce a location-referenced picture of remaining thickness over a defined area, possibly around a known corrosion mechanism.
       </p>
       <p>
        <strong>
         Methods to assess.
        </strong>
        Dedicated encoded PAUT corrosion mapping may support broad-area records on suitable surfaces. Weld-seam PAUT or TOFD can be scoped separately for weld integrity.
       </p>
       <p>
        <strong>
         Scope inputs.
        </strong>
        Asset drawing, grid and coverage area, nominal thickness, coating condition, surface access and minimum reporting requirements.
       </p>
      </article>
      <article class="expanded-card">
       <h4>
        Corrosion under insulation after access
       </h4>
       <p>
        <strong>
         Inspection question.
        </strong>
        Examine a selected surface once enough insulation or coating is removed for reliable ultrasonic coupling. Ultrasound generally cannot be assumed to inspect through arbitrary insulation.
       </p>
       <p>
        <strong>
         Methods to assess.
        </strong>
        PAUT thickness mapping or spot UT may be chosen for exposed areas. The inspection plan should define removal windows and areas that remain unexamined.
       </p>
       <p>
        <strong>
         Scope inputs.
        </strong>
        Insulation type, proposed removal pattern, surface temperature, corrosion history and the area needing thickness data.
       </p>
      </article>
      <article class="expanded-card">
       <h4>
        Suspected cracking and service-related indications
       </h4>
       <p>
        <strong>
         Inspection question.
        </strong>
        Find and characterize a specific indication in a weld or accessible component, then provide data for engineering review. Orientation and surface access matter.
       </p>
       <p>
        <strong>
         Methods to assess.
        </strong>
        PAUT beam sets can be planned for expected crack orientation; TOFD may add tip-diffraction information. TFM/FMC may help a validated targeted assessment when conventional views leave uncertainty.
       </p>
       <p>
        <strong>
         Scope inputs.
        </strong>
        Damage mechanism, indication location, prior reports, material, geometry, operating history and sizing objective.
       </p>
      </article>
     </div>
    </div>
    <div class="expanded-group wrap" id="specialist-components">
     <div class="expanded-group-head">
      <h3>
       Specialist components and sectors
      </h3>
      <p>
       Some assets need custom procedures, reference blocks, scanners or manufacturer-approved inspection methods. These are general industry applications, not a claim that IAIS is approved for every sector.
      </p>
     </div>
     <div class="expanded-grid">
      <article class="expanded-card">
       <h4>
        Wind turbine tower and structural welds
       </h4>
       <p>
        <strong>
         Inspection question.
        </strong>
        Inspect long circumferential and longitudinal welds in large fabricated structures, with attention to access, height and position tracking.
       </p>
       <p>
        <strong>
         Methods to assess.
        </strong>
        Encoded PAUT and TOFD can be considered for suitable tower welds. The project plan must address scanner guidance, surface preparation and any work-at-height requirements.
       </p>
       <p>
        <strong>
         Scope inputs.
        </strong>
        Tower section drawings, weld map, thickness, site access, inspection stage and governing specification.
       </p>
      </article>
      <article class="expanded-card">
       <h4>
        Offshore and marine welded structures
       </h4>
       <p>
        <strong>
         Inspection question.
        </strong>
        Assess accessible welds or pressure components where coatings, marine exposure and restricted access shape the execution plan.
       </p>
       <p>
        <strong>
         Methods to assess.
        </strong>
        PAUT/TOFD may be candidates for suitable welds. Any class, client or project approval must be verified for the exact work scope before it is claimed or relied upon.
       </p>
       <p>
        <strong>
         Scope inputs.
        </strong>
        Component and weld drawings, coating, access method, environmental constraints and required authority or client acceptance.
       </p>
      </article>
      <article class="expanded-card">
       <h4>
        Forgings, shafts and large machined components
       </h4>
       <p>
        <strong>
         Inspection question.
        </strong>
        Screen or investigate internal reflectors in thick solid components, where grain structure and beam penetration are important.
       </p>
       <p>
        <strong>
         Methods to assess.
        </strong>
        Straight-beam or angle-beam PAUT may be configured for a defined examination volume. FMC/TFM can be explored for targeted imaging only after suitable performance demonstration.
       </p>
       <p>
        <strong>
         Scope inputs.
        </strong>
        Forging geometry, material, heat treatment, grain characteristics, surface finish and required reflector sensitivity.
       </p>
      </article>
      <article class="expanded-card">
       <h4>
        Castings and complex-shaped parts
       </h4>
       <p>
        <strong>
         Inspection question.
        </strong>
        Examine suitable cast components where rough surfaces, variable thickness and coarse microstructure can make ultrasound difficult.
       </p>
       <p>
        <strong>
         Methods to assess.
        </strong>
        Specialized PAUT may be investigated with representative samples and sensitivity trials. No method should be promised before attenuation and geometry are assessed.
       </p>
       <p>
        <strong>
         Scope inputs.
        </strong>
        Casting process and alloy, drawing, finish, expected defect type and representative reference material.
       </p>
      </article>
      <article class="expanded-card">
       <h4>
        Composite and bonded components
       </h4>
       <p>
        <strong>
         Inspection question.
        </strong>
        Identify delamination, disbond or internal features in a material system that differs substantially from metallic welds.
       </p>
       <p>
        <strong>
         Methods to assess.
        </strong>
        Purpose-designed phased-array ultrasonic setups are used in industry for some composites. TOFD weld procedures are not transferable; TFM/FMC suitability depends on layup, attenuation and validation.
       </p>
       <p>
        <strong>
         Scope inputs.
        </strong>
        Material stack and layup, component geometry, bond design, thickness, access and acceptance specification.
       </p>
      </article>
      <article class="expanded-card">
       <h4>
        Rail and transport components
       </h4>
       <p>
        <strong>
         Inspection question.
        </strong>
        Investigate defined welds or critical components under the sector&rsquo;s own maintenance and qualification rules.
       </p>
       <p>
        <strong>
         Methods to assess.
        </strong>
        PAUT can be a candidate technology where an approved procedure and suitable equipment exist. Sector-specific approvals and demonstration must be established before offering the application.
       </p>
       <p>
        <strong>
         Scope inputs.
        </strong>
        Component type, material, inspection standard, service history, defect target and access constraints.
       </p>
      </article>
     </div>
    </div>
    <div class="wrap expanded-end">
     <p>
      <strong>
       Important distinction:
      </strong>
      these are documented technology application areas, not a statement that IAIS holds a particular sector approval or has a ready-made procedure for every component. IAIS must review each enquiry, demonstrate suitability where needed, and confirm the exact personnel, equipment, code and acceptance basis in the proposal.
     </p>
     <a class="text-link" href="#enquiry">
      Send your application details &nearrow;
     </a>
    </div>
   </section>
   <!-- ===== CONTENT SECTION 5: application-sectors wrap ===== -->
   <section class="application-sectors wrap">
    <div>
     <p class="section-kicker">
      INDUSTRY CONTEXT
     </p>
     <h2>
      Where these questions arise
     </h2>
     <p>
      Applications occur across several industries. The sector helps explain the operating environment, but it does not select the ultrasonic technique on its own.
     </p>
    </div>
    <div class="sector-grid">
     <article>
      <h3>
       Oil, gas &amp; petrochemicals
      </h3>
      <p>
       Process piping, pressure equipment and shutdown or in-service integrity questions.
      </p>
     </article>
     <article>
      <h3>
       Power generation
      </h3>
      <p>
       Fabricated welds and operating components requiring defined examination coverage.
      </p>
     </article>
     <article>
      <h3>
       Manufacturing &amp; fabrication
      </h3>
      <p>
       Weld quality verification and inspection records during production and construction.
      </p>
     </article>
     <article>
      <h3>
       Marine &amp; offshore
      </h3>
      <p>
       Accessible welded components and pressure boundaries, subject to site and governing requirements.
      </p>
     </article>
    </div>
   </section>
   <!-- ===== CONTENT SECTION 6: application-selection ===== -->
   <section class="application-selection">
    <div class="wrap">
     <p class="section-kicker">
      METHOD SELECTION
     </p>
     <h2>
      Compare the role of each method
     </h2>
     <p class="table-intro">
      This table is an orientation aid. It is not a code-compliant procedure or a guarantee of coverage.
     </p>
     <div class="table-scroll">
      <table>
       <thead>
        <tr>
         <th scope="col">
          Method
         </th>
         <th scope="col">
          Typical contribution
         </th>
         <th scope="col">
          Planning question
         </th>
        </tr>
       </thead>
       <tbody>
        <tr>
         <th scope="row">
          <a href="tofd.html">
           TOFD
          </a>
         </th>
         <td>
          Diffraction-based weld examination and sizing information in suitable geometry.
         </td>
         <td>
          Can both probes travel as required, and how are near-surface zones addressed?
         </td>
        </tr>
        <tr>
         <th scope="row">
          <a href="paut.html">
           PAUT
          </a>
         </th>
         <td>
          Controlled sound paths and scan views for welds or a dedicated mapping setup.
         </td>
         <td>
          Which probe, wedge, focal laws and scan positions demonstrate the required volume?
         </td>
        </tr>
        <tr>
         <th scope="row">
          <a href="tfm.html">
           TFM
          </a>
         </th>
         <td>
          Focused image reconstruction within a defined region of interest.
         </td>
         <td>
          Has image quality and performance been demonstrated for this material and geometry?
         </td>
        </tr>
        <tr>
         <th scope="row">
          <a href="fmc.html">
           FMC
          </a>
         </th>
         <td>
          Array data acquisition for processing such as TFM.
         </td>
         <td>
          What acquisition and processing plan produces the required evaluable result?
         </td>
        </tr>
       </tbody>
      </table>
     </div>
    </div>
   </section>
   <!-- ===== CONTENT SECTION 7: application-next wrap ===== -->
   <section class="application-next wrap">
    <div>
     <p class="section-kicker">
      FROM QUESTION TO SCOPE
     </p>
     <h2>
      What happens after you send an enquiry?
     </h2>
    </div>
    <div class="next-steps">
     <article>
      <b>
       01
      </b>
      <h3>
       Define the decision
      </h3>
      <p>
       Identify the component, suspected condition, required examination volume and the client&rsquo;s acceptance or engineering question.
      </p>
     </article>
     <article>
      <b>
       02
      </b>
      <h3>
       Review feasibility
      </h3>
      <p>
       Check material, thickness, geometry, access, applicable specification and whether a suitable calibration or validation approach is available.
      </p>
     </article>
     <article>
      <b>
       03
      </b>
      <h3>
       Agree the deliverable
      </h3>
      <p>
       Set the method, procedure, scan coverage, reporting format, personnel, equipment and schedule in the project proposal.
      </p>
     </article>
    </div>
   </section>
   <!-- ===== CONTENT SECTION 8: enquiry ===== -->
   <section class="enquiry" id="enquiry">
    <div class="wrap enquiry-grid">
     <div>
      <p class="section-kicker">
       APPLICATION ENQUIRY
      </p>

       Describe your asset and the decision you need to make.
      </h2>
      <p>
       Send drawings, material, wall thickness, suspected condition, inspection history, site location, governing specification and target dates. IAIS can review which approach is feasible for your application.
      </p>

        <input maxlength="150" name="location" placeholder="City, country or offshore location" required=""/>
       </label>
       <label>
        Target schedule
        <input maxlength="120" name="schedule" placeholder="Dates or shutdown window"/>
       </label>
      </div>
      <div class="form-row">
       <label>
        Component / asset
        <input maxlength="150" name="component" placeholder="For example, pipe girth weld or vessel seam"/>
       </label>
       <label>
        Material and thickness
        <input maxlength="150" name="material" placeholder="If known"/>
       </label>
      </div>
      <label>
       Applicable code / specification
       <input maxlength="180" name="specification" placeholder="If available"/>
      </label>
      <label>
       Inspection objective and scope
       <span aria-hidden="true">
        *
       </span>
       <textarea maxlength="1800" minlength="20" name="scope" placeholder="What needs to be inspected? Include quantity, dimensions, access, suspected condition and required result." required="" rows="6"></textarea>
      </label>
      <p class="form-hint">
       Fields marked * are required. Attach drawings, weld maps, prior reports or specifications in your email after it opens.
      </p>

       Prepare email enquiry

     <p class="eyebrow">
      FULL MATRIX CAPTURE (FMC)
     </p>
     <h1>
      FMC acquisition for advanced ultrasonic analysis.
     </h1>
     <p class="lead">
      Full Matrix Capture is an ultrasonic data acquisition approach. It records transmit-receive combinations across an array so that the data can be processed into images, including with the Total Focusing Method.
     </p>
     <a class="button primary" href="#enquire">
      Request a FMC proposal
      <span aria-hidden="true">
       &nearrow;
      </span>
     </a>
    </div>
   </section>
   <!-- ===== CONTENT SECTION 2: method-intro wrap ===== -->
   <section class="method-intro wrap">
    <div>
     <p class="section-kicker">
      HOW THE METHOD WORKS
     </p>
     <h2>
      Full Matrix Capture (FMC)
     </h2>
    </div>
    <p>
     During a typical FMC acquisition cycle, an array element transmits while the available elements receive, and the sequence repeats across the array. The resulting A-scan matrix can be processed using selected imaging algorithms. FMC is the recorded data foundation; the interpreted image and acceptance decision depend on subsequent processing and an approved method.
    </p>
   </section>
   <!-- ===== CONTENT SECTION 3: method-use ===== -->
   <section class="method-use">
    <div class="wrap">
     <p class="section-kicker">
      WHERE IT MAY HELP
     </p>
     <h2>
      Potential inspection applications
     </h2>
     <div class="method-use-grid">
      <article>
       <h3>
        TFM data collection
       </h3>
       <p>
        Acquiring suitable array data for a specified TFM reconstruction and region of interest.
       </p>
      </article>
      <article>
       <h3>
        Retained data for review
       </h3>
       <p>
        Maintaining a documented acquisition record where the project scope requires later processing or re-evaluation.
       </p>
      </article>
      <article>
       <h3>
        Method development
       </h3>
       <p>
        Evaluating acquisition settings and imaging approaches against relevant reference reflectors before an examination.
       </p>
      </article>
     </div>
     <p class="method-note">
      Applications are illustrative. Suitability depends on the component and approved inspection procedure.
     </p>
    </div>
   </section>
   <!-- ===== CONTENT SECTION 4: method-planning wrap ===== -->
   <section class="method-planning wrap">
    <div>
     <p class="section-kicker">
      SCOPE DEVELOPMENT
     </p>
     <h2>
      Information needed for a FMC enquiry
     </h2>
     <p>
      Send available drawings or specifications with the details below. IAIS can use them to review coverage, calibration, site arrangements and reporting requirements.
     </p>
    </div>
    <div class="service-checklist">
     <div>
      <b>
       01
      </b>
      <span>
       Array size, probe type and acquisition modes
      </span>
     </div>
     <div>
      <b>
       02
      </b>
      <span>
       Component dimensions, material and sound paths
      </span>
     </div>
     <div>
      <b>
       03
      </b>
      <span>
       Data volume, scanning speed and storage needs
      </span>
     </div>
     <div>
      <b>
       04
      </b>
      <span>
       Intended processing, validation and reporting requirements
      </span>
     </div>
    </div>
   </section>
   <!-- ===== CONTENT SECTION 5: method-limits ===== -->
   <section class="method-limits">
    <div class="wrap">
     <p class="section-kicker">
      TECHNICAL CONSIDERATIONS
     </p>
     <h2>
      Limits and complementary methods
     </h2>
     <p>
      FMC is an acquisition method, not a stand-alone flaw classification or acceptance criterion. Data size and capture time can increase substantially, and a useful result depends on setup, processing and qualified interpretation.
     </p>
    </div>
   </section>
   <!-- ===== CONTENT SECTION 6: related wrap ===== -->

   <section class="hero hero-cover">
    <img alt="Illustrative industrial scene of an inspector guiding an encoded ultrasonic scanner over a pipe weld beside a portable flaw detector" class="hero-cover-image" fetchpriority="high" height="768" src="assets/images/ultrasonic-pipe-weld-inspection-full-banner.webp" width="2048"/>
    <div class="wrap hero-cover-content">
     <p class="eyebrow">
      <span class="line">
      </span>
      ADVANCED ULTRASONIC INSPECTION &middot; INDIA &amp; UAE
     </p>
     <h1>
      See deeper into critical welds and assets.
     </h1>
     <p class="lead">
      TOFD, PAUT, TFM and FMC inspection services for projects where clear, traceable ultrasonic evidence matters. Plan your scope with IAIS teams in Chennai and Abu Dhabi.
     </p>
     <div class="actions">
      <a class="button primary" href="#enquiry">
       Discuss your inspection scope
       <span aria-hidden="true">
        &nearrow;
       </span>
      </a>
      <a class="button secondary" href="#methods">
       Explore the methods
       <span aria-hidden="true">
        &darr;
       </span>
      </a>
     </div>
     <p class="hero-image-note">
      ILLUSTRATIVE INSPECTION SCENE
     </p>
    </div>
   </section>
   <!-- ===== CONTENT SECTION 2: intro wrap ===== -->
   <section class="intro wrap">
    <p class="section-kicker">
     INSPECTION, BUILT AROUND THE QUESTION
    </p>
    <div class="intro-grid">
     <h2>
      Choose the technique to suit the component and inspection objective.
     </h2>
     <p>
      Advanced ultrasonic methods produce different views of material and weld conditions. The right procedure depends on geometry, material, access, applicable code, detection objective and reporting requirements. IAIS can review these inputs before proposing an inspection plan.
     </p>
    </div>
   </section>
   <!-- ===== CONTENT SECTION 3: methods | small inline SVG icons can be edited inside each card ===== -->
   <section class="methods" id="methods">
    <div class="wrap">
     <div class="section-head">
      <div>
       <p class="section-kicker">
        CORE SERVICES
       </p>
       <h2>
        Four methods. A considered inspection plan.
       </h2>
      </div>
      <p>
       Technique selection and coverage are established for each assignment.
      </p>
     </div>
     <div class="cards">
      <article class="card">
       <span class="number">
        01 / TOFD
       </span>
       <span aria-hidden="true" class="service-icon">
        <svg viewbox="0 0 24 24">
         <path d="M4 18V6m16 12V6M7 12h10M9 8l-2 4 2 4m6-8 2 4-2 4">
         </path>
        </svg>
       </span>
       <h3>
        Time of Flight Diffraction
       </h3>
       <p>
        Ultrasonic technique using diffracted signals to help locate and size discontinuities, commonly applied to weld inspection.
       </p>
       <a href="tofd.html">
        Enquire about TOFD
        <span aria-hidden="true">
         &nearrow;
        </span>
       </a>
      </article>
      <article class="card">
       <span class="number">
        02 / PAUT
       </span>
       <span aria-hidden="true" class="service-icon">
        <svg viewbox="0 0 24 24">
         <path d="M5 17V7m4 12V5m4 14V5m4 12V7M3 12h18">
         </path>
        </svg>
       </span>
       <h3>
        Phased Array Ultrasonic Testing
       </h3>
       <p>
        Electronically controlled beam angles and focal laws support encoded examination and interpretable views of inspected areas.
       </p>
       <a href="paut.html">
        Enquire about PAUT
        <span aria-hidden="true">
         &nearrow;
        </span>
       </a>
      </article>
      <article class="card">
       <span class="number">
        03 / TFM
       </span>
       <span aria-hidden="true" class="service-icon">
        <svg viewbox="0 0 24 24">
         <circle cx="12" cy="12" r="3">
         </circle>
         <path d="M4 9V5h4m8 0h4v4m0 6v4h-4m-8 0H4v-4">
         </path>
        </svg>
       </span>
       <h3>
        Total Focusing Method
       </h3>
       <p>
        An imaging and reconstruction method that uses full matrix data to focus across a defined region of interest.
       </p>
       <a href="tfm.html">
        Enquire about TFM
        <span aria-hidden="true">
         &nearrow;
        </span>
       </a>
      </article>
      <article class="card">
       <span class="number">
        04 / FMC
       </span>
       <span aria-hidden="true" class="service-icon">
        <svg viewbox="0 0 24 24">
         <path d="M5 5h4v4H5zM15 5h4v4h-4zM5 15h4v4H5zM15 15h4v4h-4zM9 7h6M7 9v6m10-6v6m-8 2h6">
         </path>
        </svg>
       </span>
       <h3>
        Full Matrix Capture
       </h3>
       <p>
        An acquisition approach that records element combinations for subsequent analysis and imaging, including TFM workflows.
       </p>
       <a href="fmc.html">
        Enquire about FMC
        <span aria-hidden="true">
         &nearrow;
        </span>
       </a>
      </article>
     </div>
     <p class="method-note">
      Method descriptions explain general technical principles. Final inspection suitability and acceptance criteria depend on the project specification and approved procedure.
     </p>
    </div>
   </section>
   <!-- ===== CONTENT SECTION 4: applications ===== -->
   <section class="applications wrap" id="applications">
    <div>
     <p class="section-kicker">
      POTENTIAL APPLICATIONS
     </p>
     <h2>
      From fabrication to in-service assessment.
     </h2>
     <p>
      Advanced ultrasonic inspection may be considered for welds, piping and other accessible industrial components. Share drawings, material details and the applicable standard so the team can assess the suitable technique and coverage.
     </p>

   <!-- ===== CONTENT SECTION 5: approach ===== -->
   <section class="approach" id="approach">
    <div class="wrap">
     <p class="section-kicker">
      HOW WE FRAME THE WORK
     </p>
     <h2>
      Clarity before mobilization.
     </h2>
     <div class="steps">
      <div>
       <span>
        01
       </span>
       <h3>
        Define the scope
       </h3>
       <p>
        Review component details, material, access, inspection objective and the governing requirements.
       </p>
      </div>
      <div>
       <span>
        02
       </span>
       <h3>
        Plan the examination
       </h3>
       <p>
        Agree on technique, calibration, scan coverage, data capture and reporting needs.
       </p>
      </div>
      <div>
       <span>
        03
       </span>
       <h3>
        Deliver useful results
       </h3>
       <p>
        Execute against the agreed procedure and provide findings in the requested reporting format.
       </p>
      </div>
     </div>
    </div>

   <!-- ===== CONTENT SECTION 1: subhero method-hero ===== -->
   <section class="subhero method-hero">
    <div class="wrap">
     <nav aria-label="Breadcrumb" class="breadcrumb">
      <a href="index.html">
       Home
      </a>
      <span aria-hidden="true">
       /
      </span>
      <a href="services.html">
       Services
      </a>
      <span aria-hidden="true">
       /
      </span>
      <span>
       PAUT
      </span>
     </nav>
     <p class="eyebrow">
      PHASED ARRAY ULTRASONIC TESTING (PAUT)
     </p>
     <h1>
      PAUT inspection with planned beam coverage and recorded data.
     </h1>
     <p class="lead">
      Phased Array Ultrasonic Testing uses an array of elements with timed excitation to steer and focus ultrasonic beams. Its scan displays help trained personnel assess indications within an agreed examination volume.
     </p>
     <a class="button primary" href="#enquire">
      Request a PAUT proposal
      <span aria-hidden="true">
       &nearrow;
      </span>
     </a>
    </div>
   </section>
   <!-- ===== CONTENT SECTION 2: method-intro wrap ===== -->
   <section class="method-intro wrap">
    <div>
     <p class="section-kicker">
      HOW THE METHOD WORKS
     </p>
     <h2>
      Phased Array Ultrasonic Testing (PAUT)
     </h2>
    </div>
    <p>
     A PAUT setup defines probe, wedge, focal laws, beam angles, scan positions and calibration. Sectorial scans can show multiple angles in a cross-sectional view, while encoded scanning can link the ultrasonic data to distance along a weld or component. The technique and scan plan must demonstrate the coverage needed for the specified inspection objective.
    </p>
   </section>
   <!-- ===== CONTENT SECTION 3: method-use ===== -->
   <section class="method-use">
    <div class="wrap">
     <p class="section-kicker">
      WHERE IT MAY HELP
     </p>
     <h2>
      Potential inspection applications
     </h2>
     <div class="method-use-grid">
      <article>
       <h3>
        Weld examination
       </h3>
       <p>
        Examining accessible weld volumes in fabrication or service where material and geometry are suitable.
       </p>
      </article>
      <article>
       <h3>
        Component evaluation
       </h3>
       <p>
        Investigating defined areas of piping and other industrial components.
       </p>
      </article>
      <article>
       <h3>
        Complementary coverage
       </h3>
       <p>
        Pairing PAUT with TOFD when different sound paths and indication responses are useful under the approved procedure.
       </p>
      </article>
     </div>
     <p class="method-note">
      Applications are illustrative. Suitability depends on the component and approved inspection procedure.
     </p>
    </div>
   </section>
   <!-- ===== CONTENT SECTION 4: method-planning wrap ===== -->
   <section class="method-planning wrap">
    <div>
     <p class="section-kicker">
      SCOPE DEVELOPMENT
     </p>
     <h2>
      Information needed for a PAUT enquiry
     </h2>
     <p>
      Send available drawings or specifications with the details below. IAIS can use them to review coverage, calibration, site arrangements and reporting requirements.
     </p>
    </div>
    <div class="service-checklist">
     <div>
      <b>
       01
      </b>
      <span>
       Component material, thickness and weld geometry
      </span>
     </div>
     <div>
      <b>
       02
      </b>
      <span>
       Inspection surface, access and scanning clearance
      </span>
     </div>
     <div>
      <b>
       03
      </b>
      <span>
       Applicable code, examination volume and acceptance criteria
      </span>
     </div>
     <div>
      <b>
       04
      </b>
      <span>
       Calibration blocks, probe configuration and reporting format
      </span>
     </div>
    </div>
   </section>
   <!-- ===== CONTENT SECTION 5: method-limits ===== -->
   <section class="method-limits">
    <div class="wrap">
     <p class="section-kicker">
      TECHNICAL CONSIDERATIONS
     </p>
     <h2>
      Limits and complementary methods
     </h2>
     <p>
      Beam coverage and resolution vary with the probe, material, geometry and chosen focal laws. Coarse-grained or highly attenuating material, surface condition and restricted access may limit effectiveness. Data interpretation and acceptance remain tied to the approved procedure.
     </p>
    </div>
   </section>
   <!-- ===== CONTENT SECTION 6: related wrap ===== -->
   <section class="related wrap">

   <!-- ===== CONTENT SECTION 1: subhero services-hero ===== -->
   <section class="subhero services-hero">
    <div class="wrap">
     <nav aria-label="Breadcrumb" class="breadcrumb">
      <a href="index.html">
       Home
      </a>
      <span aria-hidden="true">
       /
      </span>
      <span>
       Services
      </span>
     </nav>
     <p class="eyebrow">
      ADVANCED ULTRASONIC SERVICES
     </p>
     <h1>
      Inspection methods selected for the question at hand.
     </h1>
     <p class="lead">
      TOFD, PAUT, TFM and FMC provide different ways to acquire and interpret ultrasonic information. IAIS reviews the component and governing requirements before recommending a procedure and reporting scope.
     </p>
     <a class="button primary" href="#request">
      Discuss your project
      <span aria-hidden="true">
       &nearrow;
      </span>
     </a>
    </div>
   </section>
   <!-- ===== CONTENT SECTION 2: service-intro wrap ===== -->
   <section class="service-intro wrap">
    <div>
     <p class="section-kicker">
      SERVICES AT A GLANCE
     </p>
     <h2>
      Choose the right evidence for the right decision.
     </h2>
    </div>
    <p>
     These techniques are related, but they are not interchangeable. TOFD focuses on diffracted signals; PAUT steers and focuses beams electronically; FMC records a full set of transmit-receive combinations; and TFM reconstructs an image from suitable FMC data. Coverage, sensitivity and acceptance criteria are established for each application.
    </p>
   </section>
   <!-- ===== CONTENT SECTION 3: service-list ===== -->
   <section aria-label="Inspection methods" class="service-list">
    <div class="wrap">
     <article class="service-row" id="tofd">
      <div class="service-code">
       01 / TOFD
      </div>
      <div>
       <h2>
        Time of Flight Diffraction
       </h2>
       <p>
        TOFD uses a transmitting and receiving probe arrangement to record lateral, back-wall and diffracted signals. It is often used for weld examination and through-wall sizing of suitable indications.
       </p>
       <p class="service-context">
        <strong>
         Scope considerations:
        </strong>
        weld geometry, wall thickness, access on both sides, surface condition, reference blocks and the applicable procedure.
       </p>
      </div>
      <a aria-label="Explore TOFD inspection service" href="tofd.html">
       Explore TOFD &nearrow;
      </a>
     </article>
     <article class="service-row" id="paut">
      <div class="service-code">
       02 / PAUT
      </div>
      <div>
       <h2>
        Phased Array Ultrasonic Testing
       </h2>
       <p>
        PAUT uses multiple probe elements and controlled delays to form, steer or focus ultrasonic beams. Sectorial and other scan views can support weld examination, mapping and indication evaluation where the technique is suitable.
       </p>
       <p class="service-context">
        <strong>
         Scope considerations:
        </strong>
        material, weld profile, probe and wedge selection, calibration, scan plan, encoding and coverage.
       </p>
      </div>
      <a aria-label="Explore PAUT inspection service" href="paut.html">
       Explore PAUT &nearrow;
      </a>
     </article>
     <article class="service-row" id="tfm">
      <div class="service-code">
       03 / TFM
      </div>
      <div>
       <h2>
        Total Focusing Method
       </h2>
       <p>
        TFM reconstructs an ultrasonic image by applying focusing calculations across a defined region of interest. Its use depends on the acquisition setup, material properties, image quality and the project&rsquo;s evaluation requirements.
       </p>
       <p class="service-context">
        <strong>
         Scope considerations:
        </strong>
        region of interest, probe setup, sound paths, material response, validation and reporting method.
       </p>
      </div>
      <a aria-label="Explore TFM inspection service" href="tfm.html">
       Explore TFM &nearrow;
      </a>
     </article>
     <article class="service-row" id="fmc">
      <div class="service-code">
       04 / FMC
      </div>
      <div>
       <h2>
        Full Matrix Capture
       </h2>
       <p>
        FMC is a data acquisition process in which array elements transmit and receive in combinations. The captured data can be processed for imaging methods such as TFM. FMC describes the acquisition, rather than a stand-alone acceptance result.
       </p>
       <p class="service-context">
        <strong>
         Scope considerations:
        </strong>
        instrument capability, array configuration, data volume, acquisition time and subsequent processing.
       </p>
      </div>
      <a aria-label="Explore FMC inspection service" href="fmc.html">
       Explore FMC &nearrow;
      </a>
     </article>
    </div>
   </section>
   <!-- ===== CONTENT SECTION 4: service-planning wrap ===== -->
   <section class="service-planning wrap">
    <div>
     <p class="section-kicker">
      PLANNING THE EXAMINATION
     </p>
     <h2>
      What goes into an inspection proposal?
     </h2>
     <p>
      A useful proposal starts with an inspection objective, not just a method name. Combining methods may be appropriate when the approved procedure and technical requirements call for complementary coverage.
     </p>
    </div>
    <div class="service-checklist">
     <div>
      <b>
       01
      </b>
      <span>
       Component drawings, weld details and dimensions
      </span>
     </div>
     <div>
      <b>
       02
      </b>
      <span>
       Material, thickness and surface/access conditions
      </span>
     </div>
     <div>
      <b>
       03
      </b>
      <span>
       Relevant code, specification and acceptance criteria
      </span>
     </div>
     <div>
      <b>
       04
      </b>
      <span>
       Location, schedule and reporting requirements
      </span>
     </div>
    </div>
   </section>
   <!-- ===== CONTENT SECTION 5: service-note ===== -->
   <section class="service-note">
    <div class="wrap">
     <strong>
      Technical note
     </strong>
     <p>
      These explanations describe general method principles. The presence of a technique on this site does not imply suitability for every material or geometry. The project procedure, calibration, personnel and acceptance basis are agreed before execution.
     </p>
    </div>
   </section>
   <!-- ===== CONTENT SECTION 6: request ===== -->

      <p>

      </p>

   <section class="subhero method-hero">
    <div class="wrap">
     <nav aria-label="Breadcrumb" class="breadcrumb">
      <a href="index.html">
       Home
      </a>
      <span aria-hidden="true">
       /

       /
      </span>
      <span>
       TFM
      </span>
     </nav>
     <p class="eyebrow">
      TOTAL FOCUSING METHOD (TFM)
     </p>
     <h1>
      TFM imaging for defined ultrasonic inspection objectives.
     </h1>
     <p class="lead">
      The Total Focusing Method reconstructs an ultrasonic image by focusing across a selected region of interest. It commonly uses Full Matrix Capture data and requires a suitable acquisition setup, material model and evaluation procedure.
     </p>
     <a class="button primary" href="#enquire">
      Request a TFM proposal
      <span aria-hidden="true">
       &nearrow;
      </span>
     </a>
    </div>
   </section>
   <!-- ===== CONTENT SECTION 2: method-intro wrap ===== -->
   <section class="method-intro wrap">
    <div>
     <p class="section-kicker">
      HOW THE METHOD WORKS
     </p>
     <h2>
      Total Focusing Method (TFM)
     </h2>
    </div>
    <p>
     In a typical FMC/TFM workflow, an array acquires transmit-receive data from its elements. TFM processing applies calculated travel times to create an image for the chosen inspection region. The quality and meaning of that image depend on probe configuration, sound paths, material properties, calibration and validation.
    </p>
   </section>
   <!-- ===== CONTENT SECTION 3: method-use ===== -->
   <section class="method-use">
    <div class="wrap">
     <p class="section-kicker">
      WHERE IT MAY HELP
     </p>
     <h2>
      Potential inspection applications
     </h2>
     <div class="method-use-grid">
      <article>
       <h3>
        Detailed indication evaluation
       </h3>
       <p>
        Reviewing the position and character of relevant indications where the method has been validated for the component.
       </p>
      </article>
      <article>
       <h3>
        Complex examination questions
       </h3>
       <p>
        Assessing whether focused images can provide additional information beyond a conventional PAUT presentation.
       </p>
      </article>
      <article>
       <h3>
        Procedure-supported comparison
       </h3>
       <p>
        Using TFM alongside other ultrasonic methods when the project procedure calls for complementary evidence.
       </p>
      </article>
     </div>
     <p class="method-note">
      Applications are illustrative. Suitability depends on the component and approved inspection procedure.
     </p>
    </div>
   </section>
   <!-- ===== CONTENT SECTION 4: method-planning wrap ===== -->
   <section class="method-planning wrap">
    <div>
     <p class="section-kicker">
      SCOPE DEVELOPMENT
     </p>
     <h2>
      Information needed for a TFM enquiry
     </h2>
     <p>
      Send available drawings or specifications with the details below. IAIS can use them to review coverage, calibration, site arrangements and reporting requirements.
     </p>
    </div>
    <div class="service-checklist">
     <div>
      <b>
       01
      </b>
      <span>
       Region of interest and expected indication locations
      </span>
     </div>
     <div>
      <b>
       02
      </b>
      <span>
       Material properties, geometry and probe access
      </span>
     </div>
     <div>
      <b>
       03
      </b>
      <span>
       Required acquisition modes, calibration and validation
      </span>
     </div>
     <div>
      <b>
       04
      </b>
      <span>
       Code or client requirements for evaluation and reporting
      </span>
     </div>
    </div>
   </section>
   <!-- ===== CONTENT SECTION 5: method-limits ===== -->
   <section class="method-limits">
    <div class="wrap">
     <p class="section-kicker">
      TECHNICAL CONSIDERATIONS
     </p>
     <h2>
      Limits and complementary methods
     </h2>
     <p>
      A visually detailed image does not by itself prove detection performance or acceptance. Focusing quality can change with velocity assumptions, sound paths, attenuation and region size. Method selection and validation should precede field use.
     </p>
    </div>
   </section>
   <!-- ===== CONTENT SECTION 6: related wrap ===== -->

   <section class="subhero method-hero">
    <div class="wrap">
     <nav aria-label="Breadcrumb" class="breadcrumb">
      <a href="index.html">
       Home
      </a>
      <span aria-hidden="true">

      <span>
       TOFD
      </span>
     </nav>
     <p class="eyebrow">
      TIME OF FLIGHT DIFFRACTION (TOFD)
     </p>
     <h1>
      TOFD inspection for weld examination and indication sizing.
     </h1>
     <p class="lead">
      Time of Flight Diffraction records the travel time of sound waves diffracted from discontinuity tips. It can support detection and through-wall sizing in suitable welds when the scan setup and procedure are matched to the component.
     </p>
     <a class="button primary" href="#enquire">
      Request a TOFD proposal
      <span aria-hidden="true">
       &nearrow;
      </span>
     </a>
    </div>
   </section>
   <!-- ===== CONTENT SECTION 2: method-intro wrap ===== -->
   <section class="method-intro wrap">
    <div>
     <p class="section-kicker">
      HOW THE METHOD WORKS
     </p>
     <h2>
      Time of Flight Diffraction (TOFD)
     </h2>
    </div>
    <p>
     A transmitting probe sends an ultrasonic beam across the weld while a receiving probe collects the signals. A typical TOFD record includes the lateral wave, back-wall reflection and, when present, signals diffracted from relevant discontinuities. Encoded movement along the weld produces a position-linked data record for interpretation.
    </p>
   </section>
   <!-- ===== CONTENT SECTION 3: method-use ===== -->
   <section class="method-use">
    <div class="wrap">
     <p class="section-kicker">
      WHERE IT MAY HELP
     </p>
     <h2>
      Potential inspection applications
     </h2>
     <div class="method-use-grid">
      <article>
       <h3>
        Fabrication welds
       </h3>
       <p>
        Examining welds where geometry and access permit the required probe arrangement and scan coverage.
       </p>
      </article>
      <article>
       <h3>
        In-service weld assessment
       </h3>
       <p>
        Collecting data to support investigation of relevant indications in accessible components.
       </p>
      </article>
      <article>
       <h3>
        Indication sizing
       </h3>
       <p>
        Using diffracted signals to estimate through-wall extent when signal quality and procedure allow it.
       </p>
      </article>
     </div>
     <p class="method-note">
      Applications are illustrative. Suitability depends on the component and approved inspection procedure.
     </p>
    </div>
   </section>
   <!-- ===== CONTENT SECTION 4: method-planning wrap ===== -->
   <section class="method-planning wrap">
    <div>
     <p class="section-kicker">
      SCOPE DEVELOPMENT
     </p>
     <h2>
      Information needed for a TOFD enquiry
     </h2>
     <p>
      Send available drawings or specifications with the details below. IAIS can use them to review coverage, calibration, site arrangements and reporting requirements.
     </p>
    </div>
    <div class="service-checklist">
     <div>
      <b>
       01
      </b>
      <span>
       Weld drawing, joint profile and nominal wall thickness
      </span>
     </div>
     <div>
      <b>
       02
      </b>
      <span>
       Material and surface condition on both sides of the weld
      </span>
     </div>
     <div>
      <b>
       03
      </b>
      <span>
       Required scan length, access and any obstructions
      </span>
     </div>
     <div>
      <b>
       04
      </b>
      <span>
       Applicable code, acceptance criteria and reference block requirements
      </span>
     </div>
    </div>
   </section>
   <!-- ===== CONTENT SECTION 5: method-limits ===== -->
   <section class="method-limits">
    <div class="wrap">
     <p class="section-kicker">
      TECHNICAL CONSIDERATIONS
     </p>
     <h2>
      Limits and complementary methods
     </h2>
     <p>
      TOFD can have reduced sensitivity close to the scanning surface and back wall. Complex geometry, attenuation, access and noise can affect interpretation. A complementary technique such as PAUT may be considered where the approved inspection plan requires it.
     </p>
    </div>
   </section>
   <!-- ===== CONTENT SECTION 6: related wrap ===== -->
   <section class="related wrap">

========================================================================================
